Vague or abstract answer choices","type":null,"visible":true}],"visibleCount":1,"truncated":false}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 's about a pool, a library or a dentist. The answer choices never do. They talk about "a premise," "a claim," "the conclusion."\n    - heading "See one" [level=3]\n    - generic: The argument\n    - list:\n      - button "(1)The town should keep the public pool open through September.":\n        - generic: (1)\n        - text: The town should keep the public pool open through September.\n      - button "(2)More families now stay in town into early fall.":\n        - generic: (2)\n        - text: More families now stay in town into early fall.\n      - button "(3)After all, the school year now starts two weeks later than it used to.":\n        - generic: (3)\n        - text: After all, the school year now starts two weeks later than it used to.\n    - generic: Answer choice\n    - generic: It is supported by another statement, and it supports the main conclusion.\n    - paragraph: Click the sentence this answer choice is describing.\n    - heading "Why they feel hard" [level=3]\n    - paragraph:\n      - strong: 1. Everyday words, strict meanings.\n      - text: On the LSAT, "explanation" means cause and "presuppose" means require. Read them the everyday way and you misread the choice.\n    - paragraph:\n      - strong: 2. Nothing to hold onto.\n      - text: There is no pool in the answer choice. You have to supply the content yourself by going back to the stimulus.\n    - paragraph:\n      - strong: 3. One word can sink it.\n      - text: A choice can describe the argument perfectly except for one word, like "proves" where the author only "suggests." That one word makes it wrong.\n    - heading "How this bootcamp fixes it" [level=3]\n    - paragraph: First the words (Module 2). Then a routine for breaking any abstract choice into plain English (Module 3) and matching every piece to the stimulus (Module 4). Then methods and traps (Modules 5 and 6). Module 7 puts it all together.\n    - generic: An abstract choice is a description. Your job is to find what it describes.\n    - heading "Answer Choice Traps" [level=3]\n    - paragraph: For the hardest LR and RC questions, read each answer choice carefully. Look for suspicious keywords, concepts and connections that seem out of place. These three types show why that habit matters.\n    - heading "Type 1. Half right, half wrong" [level=3]\n    - paragraph: A choice can be only partially correct. The first half may match, while the second half adds something outside the scope of the argument. Read the whole answer word by word. One correct part doesn\'t make the rest correct.\n    - heading "Type 2. Describing something that did not happen" [level=3]\n    - paragraph:\n      - text: "Role, Method and Flaw answers describe a feature of the stimulus\'s argument. A choice that describes something the author never does can be eliminated. Read it, think about what it means, isolate any suspicious words and ask:"\n      - strong: does the author do this in the argument?\n    - paragraph: Keep the question type in mind. Strengthen and Weaken answers can introduce information that isn\'t in the stimulus. For those questions, being new information doesn\'t make an answer wrong.\n    - region "Type 3. Vague or abstract answer choices":\n      - heading "Type 3. Vague or abstract answer choices" [level=3]\n      - paragraph: The correct answer can be vaguely worded. That makes it harder to recognize what the choice is referring to, but vagueness alone isn\'t a reason to eliminate it.\n      - paragraph: Extract the keywords and think about what they mean on the LSAT. A noun names a concept you need to find in the stimulus. A verb names something the author needs to do. An adjective or adverb needs to describe the argument\'s tone or strength correctly.\n      - paragraph: Then read the choice again and match what it describes to the stimulus\'s structure. Go back and forth as often as you need. This skill gets easier with practice, but rushing past the keywords gives you less practice at the very thing you need to learn.\n      - paragraph: Module 3 gives you the seven steps on their own page before the drill. You\'ll use them to slow down, pull out the keywords and say what each choice means.\n      - paragraph: Watch for an almost perfect answer with one word that makes it wrong, sitting beside a correct answer that\'s more vaguely worded. Check the word against the stimulus. The more specific-sounding answer doesn\'t win just because it\'s easier to picture.\n  - button "I read this":\n    - text: I read this\n  - generic: Click when done'}]

Put It Together\n    - button "Final Set":\n      - generic: Final Set\n    - button "Reference":\n      - text: Reference\n- main:\n  - button "Module 5":\n    - text: Module 5\n  - \'heading "Note: Spot the Method" [level=2]\'\n  - paragraph: Name what the author does, not what the author says.\n  - article:\n    - generic: A Method question asks what the author does. The right answer describes the author\'s move, not the topic.\n    - paragraph:\n      - text: "Break each choice down exactly as in Module 3. Then ask one question:"\n      - strong: did the author do this?\n      - text: Not "is this true about the topic." Did the author make this move, in this argument?\n    - heading "The seventeen moves" [level=3]\n    - paragraph: Almost every method answer describes one of these. They fall into four families.\n    - paragraph: Arguing about causes\n    - list:\n      - listitem: Offering an alternative explanation\n      - listitem: Proving one cause by ruling out the others\n      - listitem: Showing the opponent\'s evidence fits another explanation too\n      - listitem: Inferring a cause from a correlation\n    - paragraph: Leaning on an outside source\n    - list:\n      - listitem: Appealing to a definition\n      - listitem: Appealing to an authority\n    - paragraph: Going after the other side\n    - list:\n      - listitem: Pointing out undesirable consequences of a proposal\n      - listitem: Pointing out information the opponent overlooked\n      - listitem: Attacking a premise of the opponent\'s argument\n      - listitem: Exposing an erroneous assumption\n      - listitem: Showing the conclusion leads to an absurd result\n      - listitem: Using a counterexample against a general claim\n      - listitem: Criticizing an inappropriate comparison\n    - paragraph: Moving between rules and cases\n    - list:\n      - listitem: Deriving a general principle from specific cases\n      - listitem: Applying a generalization to a specific case\n      - listitem: Inferring that two groups share a trait because they share others\n      - listitem: Using an analogy\n    - paragraph: Each one has a worked example in the Methods Library in the sidebar.\n    - heading "What a method answer is not" [level=3]\n    - text: Wrong\n    - generic: A description of the topic. "The argument discusses school funding" names a subject, not a move.\n    - text: Wrong\n    - generic: A move the author could have made. If the author never compares two things, "uses an analogy" is wrong, however reasonable it sounds.\n    - text: Right\n    - generic: The move the author actually made. You should be able to point to the sentences where it happens.\n  - button "I read this":\n    - text: I read this\n  - generic: Click when done'}]

Put It Together\n    - button "Final Set":\n      - generic: Final Set\n    - button "Reference":\n      - text: Reference\n- main:\n  - button "Module 6":\n    - text: Module 6\n  - \'heading "Note: Catch the Traps" [level=2]\'\n  - paragraph: Wrong role, wrong method, structural mismatch, one-word traps.\n  - article:\n    - generic: Most wrong answers to Role, Method and Flaw questions fall into four traps. Name the trap and the answer is easy to throw out.\n    - paragraph: 1. Wrong role\n    - paragraph: Gets the job of the statement wrong.\n    - paragraph: Calls a premise the main conclusion, or calls the author\'s own support a concession.\n    - paragraph: 2. Wrong method\n    - paragraph: Describes a move the author never makes.\n    - paragraph: Says "offers an analogy" when nothing is compared.\n    - paragraph: 3. Structural mismatch\n    - paragraph: Names a logical structure the argument doesn\'t have.\n    - paragraph: Says "confuses necessary and sufficient" when there is no requirement in the argument, or "mistakes time order for cause" when nothing depends on time.\n    - paragraph: 4. Attractive language\n    - paragraph: Close to right, but one word is too strong or out of scope.\n    - paragraph: "\\"Proves\\" for \\"suggests.\\" \\"Always\\" for \\"often.\\" \\"False\\" for \\"not shown.\\""\n    - heading "The one-word trap" [level=3]\n    - paragraph: This is attractive language at its sneakiest. The answer describes the argument almost perfectly, except for one word. It often sits right next to the correct answer, which tends to be worded more cautiously.\n    - generic: Stimulus\n    - text: The coach credits the new diet for the team\'s faster race times. But the team also started training at altitude this season.\n    - text: Wrong\n    - generic: It proves that the diet had no effect. The author only points to another possible cause. "Proves" and "no effect" are far too strong.\n    - text: Right\n    - generic: It points to another possible cause of the faster times. Cautious, and every piece matches.\n    - paragraph: When two answers look nearly the same, read them word by word. Find the word that\'s different and ask whether the stimulus earns it.\n  - button "I read this":\n    - text: I read this\n  - generic: Click when done'}]

An explanation is a cause.\n    - generic: Undermine\n    - paragraph: Weaken. Push against.\n    - generic: Defend\n    - paragraph: Argue that a position is right.\n    - paragraph: Saying why someone holds a view is not defending it.\n    - paragraph: When one of these shows up in an answer choice, swap in the strict meaning before you go back to the stimulus. ## assistant

My recommendation is three main stages: understand the words, decode the 15 choices, then apply the meaning to an argument. Keep the methods library as optional reference, fold traps into the introduction and feedback, and remove M7’s duplicate quiz layer.

The Final Set also lacks answer explanations and prevents reviewing earlier questions after submission. The confusion comes from both the course structure and the assessment itself.

## assistant

**I would reduce this to three stages and remove the current Final Set from the student path.** I inspected the running preview and audited the content behind all seven modules.

They contain material, but the structure is fragmented. I rebuilt M3 without resolving the surrounding sequence, which left the course feeling inconsistent.

| Current section | What I recommend |
|---|---|
| **M1: Why Abstract Is Hard** | Combine with M2 into one introduction: what abstract choices are, why they’re difficult, and the words students need. |
| **M2: LSAT Words** | Keep its teaching and useful vocabulary checks within that introduction. Keep the word bank available during practice. |
| **M3: Break It Down** | Keep as the core: seven-step instructions followed by the 15-choice drill. |
| **M4: Match to the Stimulus** | Keep as the next stage, but make students actually identify where each part of the choice appears in the argument. The full-question drill currently asks ordinary multiple-choice questions. |
| **M5: Spot the Method** | Move to optional reference. Seventeen argument methods are too much of a detour here. Three quiz items also present descriptions of methods instead of actual arguments. |
| **M6: Catch the Traps** | Fold its useful material into the introduction and practice feedback. It doesn’t need another chapter. |
| **M7: Put It Together** | Remove the separate module. Seven of its ten decoding choices repeat M3, despite promising fresh choices. Preserve useful additional questions for later practice. |

The student’s path would become:

1. **Understand abstract answer choices.**
2. **Decode the 15 choices into plain English.**
3. **Apply that meaning to an argument and justify the match.**

That gives every stage a clear purpose and makes the next action obvious.

**The Final Set needs to come out for now.** It contains five synthetic questions about:

- The printing press and scientific revolution.
- Stretching and sports injuries.
- A magazine and the mayor’s privacy.
- Corporate taxes and a politician’s employment.
- Government compared to a machine.

They are mixed Role, Method and Flaw practice under a 7:30 timer. The first question asks about the **opponent’s argument**, but its keyed answer describes the **author’s rebuttal**. Another has a questionable “generalization” answer. There are no answer explanations, and submission prevents reviewing earlier questions. [Final Set source](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu-abstract/src/topics/abstract/components/views/TimedFinalSet.tsx:7)

The highest-value change is consolidating the navigation and removing duplicate practice. Repairing applied practice comes next. A replacement final assessment should wait until we have reviewed questions that test the skill this course teaches.

I haven’t changed anything in this audit. These cuts would preserve source material and existing student records.

## My request:
tell me whtich is acutally useful to cut out any flufff


## assistant

**Keep M1–M3 as the core. Keep M4 only after improving its practice. Cut M5–M7 from the required sequence and remove the current Final Set.**

| Section | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **M1: Why Abstract Is Hard** | Keep, combine with M2 | Gives students the reason for the skill and a concrete example. |
| **M2: LSAT Words** | Keep | Students need these meanings to decode choices. Keep the vocabulary accessible during practice. |
| **M3: Break It Down** | Keep | The 15-choice drill directly trains the main skill: extracting keywords and explaining the whole choice. |
| **M4: Match to the Stimulus** | Keep, repair | Essential next step: prove that every part of the choice matches the argument. Its practice needs to require that explicitly. |
| **M5: Spot the Method** | Move to optional reference | Seventeen methods expand this into another course. Useful reference, unnecessary required chapter. |
| **M6: Catch the Traps** | Fold into feedback | Keep the one-word and wrong-description lessons. A separate module repeats material already taught. |
| **M7: Put It Together** | Cut the module | Mostly recap. Seven of ten decoding choices repeat M3. Preserve useful fresh questions for applied practice. |
| **Final Set** | Remove for now | Flawed questions, no explanations, and poor review access. It does not provide a dependable final check. |

The lean course should be **Understand → Decode → Match**.

Also cut the separate four-step routines, repeated recaps, and the automatic **Note → Quiz → Drill** structure where it adds no distinct learning task. Use your seven-step procedure consistently.

These are recommendations; I haven’t made the cuts.

--bg:#ebe8df;\\r\\n    --line:#c7c0b3;\\r\\n    --soft:#f1eee6;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  *{box-sizing:border-box}\\r\\n  body{\\r\\n    margin:0;\\r\\n    background:var(--bg);\\r\\n    color:var(--ink);\\r\\n    font-family:Georgia,\\"Times New Roman\\",serif;\\r\\n    line-height:1.58;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  main{\\r\\n    max-width:900px;\\r\\n    margin:32px auto;\\r\\n    background:var(--paper);\\r\\n    padding:54px 62px 72px;\\r\\n    box-shadow:0 8px 28px rgba(0,0,0,.08);\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  h1,h2,h3{\\r\\n    font-family:Arial,Helvetica,sans-serif;\\r\\n    line-height:1.2;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  h1{\\r\\n    margin:0 0 1.8rem;\\r\\n    font-size:2rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .section{\\r\\n    margin:2.6rem 0;\\r\\n    padding-top:1rem;\\r\\n    border-top:1px solid var(--line);\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .section-number{\\r\\n    font-family:Arial,Helvetica,sans-serif;\\r\\n    font-size:.8rem;\\r\\n    font-weight:700;\\r\\n    letter-spacing:.12em;\\r\\n    text-transform:uppercase;\\r\\n    color:#666;\\r\\n    margin-bottom:.4rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  h2{\\r\\n    margin:.2rem 0 1rem;\\r\\n    font-size:1.4rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  p{margin:.8rem 0}\\r\\n  .focus{\\r\\n    margin:1.8rem 0;\\r\\n    padding:22px 24px;\\r\\n    border:2px solid #222;\\r\\n    background:var(--soft);\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .focus h3{\\r\\n    margin:0 0 1rem;\\r\\n    font-size:1.05rem;\\r\\n    letter-spacing:.03em;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .focus ol{\\r\\n    margin:0;\\r\\n    padding-left:1.45rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .focus li{\\r\\n    margin:.7rem 0;\\r\\n    padding-left:.25rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .em{\\r\\n    font-weight:700;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .underline{\\r\\n    text-decoration:underline;\\r\\n    text-underline-offset:2px;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .final{\\r\\n    margin-top:1.4rem;\\r\\n    padding-top:1rem;\\r\\n    border-top:1px solid var(--line);\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  @media(max-width:700px){\\r\\n    main{margin:0;padding:28px 20px 48px}\\r\\n  }\\r\\n</style>\\r\\n</head>\\r\\n<body>\\r\\n<main>\\r\\n<h1>Answer Choice Traps</h1>\\r\\n\\r\\n<section class=\\"section\\">\\r\\n  <div class=\\"section-number\\">Answer Choice Type 1</div>\\r\\n  <h2>Half right half wrong answers</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <p><strong>An essential habit to have for the hardest LR questions (and RC questions) is to carefully parse the answer choices, looking for suspicious keywords or concepts and connections that seem out of place.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <p>The test makers love to offer us an answer choice that is only partially correct. The first half of the answer may be correct, but the second half will contain out of scope information, for instance. So it’s crucial to review each answer word by word, looking for red flags.</p>\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n\\r\\n<section class=\\"section\\">\\r\\n  <div class=\\"section-number\\">Answer Choice Type 2</div>\\r\\n  <h2>Answers describing something that did not happen</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <p>For questions whose correct answer describes a feature of the stimuli’s argument (Role, Method, Flaw), we need to keep a watchful eye for answer choices describing something that does not happen in the argument.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <p>In fact, identifying answer choices that deviate from the stimulus is a sure-fire way to eliminate erroneous contenders from the selection. For these four types of questions, read the answer choice, think about what it means, <span class=\\"underline\\">isolate any suspicious words in them</span>, and ask yourself, “does the author do this in their argument?”</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <p>But of course, this depends on the question type we are working on. Similar to Out of Scope answer choices, answer choices describing something that does not occur in the stimulus can work as correct Strengthen/Weaken answers, among others.</p>\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n\\r\\n<section class=\\"section\\">\\r\\n  <div class=\\"section-number\\">Answer Choice Type 3</div>\\r\\n  <h2>Answer choices that are too vague/abstract</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <p>Finally, we have seen how the correct answer can be vaguely worded. We have even practiced the art of extrapolating what an answer is referring to by analyzing its keywords.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <p>Extracting certain keywords from vague or abstract answer choices in order to understand what that answer choice is actually talking about is also an invaluable skill. Unfortunately, it’s one of the most under-developed skills and often overlooked when students pursue speed at the expense of accuracy.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <div class=\\"focus\\">\\r\\n    <h3>When faced with an abstract answer choice, this is what we do:</h3>\\r\\n    <ol>\\r\\n      <li>Read the answer choice over, <strong><u>extract any keywords</u></strong> that stand out to you.</li>\\r\\n      <li>Think what these keywords mean in a LSAT specific setting.</li>\\r\\n      <li>If it’s a <strong>noun</strong>, ask yourself, does this concept appear in the stimulus?</li>\\r\\n      <li>If it’s a <strong>verb</strong>, ask yourself, does the author do this in the argument?</li>\\r\\n      <li>If it’s an <strong>adjective/adverb</strong>, does it correctly describe the tone or strength of the author’s argument?</li>\\r\\n      <li><u>Read the answer choice again</u> and see if you can match up what the answer is describing with the stimulus’ structure.</li>\\r\\n      <li><strong><u>Don’t be afraid to go back and forth</u></strong> between the answer choice and the stimulus, the more you practice this skill, the easier it will be.</li>\\r\\n    </ol>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <p class=\\"final\\">Lastly, a common trick employed by the authors of the test is to have an almost perfect answer with a glaring error, usually due to one single word placed right next to the correct answer, which is worded vaguely. This is a trap that we shall see over and over again throughout the book.</p>\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n\\r\\n</main>\\r\\n</body>\\r\\n</html>\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': .bold{font-weight:700}\\r\\n  .rule{\\r\\n    border:0;\\r\\n    border-top:1px solid var(--line);\\r\\n    margin:2.4rem 0;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n\\r\\n  .exercise{\\r\\n    margin:2.5rem 0 3.2rem;\\r\\n    padding-top:.2rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .exercise-label{\\r\\n    font-family:Arial,Helvetica,sans-serif;\\r\\n    font-size:.8rem;\\r\\n    font-weight:700;\\r\\n    letter-spacing:.11em;\\r\\n padding:18px 20px;\\r\\n    margin-bottom:1rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .question-label,\\r\\n  .breakdown-label{\\r\\n    font-family:Arial,Helvetica,sans-serif;\\r\\n    font-size:.78rem;\\r\\n    font-weight:700;\\r\\n    letter-spacing:.08em;\\r\\n    text-transform:uppercase;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .question-label{color:var(--muted);margin-bottom:.45rem;}\\r\\n  .question-text{\\r\\n [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 5.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"9a8c29","wall_time_seconds":0.4459616,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3683,"output":"</style>\\r\\n</head>\\r\\n<body>\\r\\n<main>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h1>Role Question Answer Choices (The Hardest Thing about Role Questions)</h1><div class=\\"source-note\\">Exercise format: each answer choice appears first as the question, followed immediately by its full breakdown and examples.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>While the way in which we read the stimulus of any Role question is similar to how we approach Find the Conclusion questions, the answer choices we are faced with in Role questions are much more difficult. Life would be so simple if all the answer choices were just asking us if the statement in question is a premise or conclusion, but the test makers love to describe a relatively straightforward concept in abstract and vague language in Role question answer choices.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>As a result, the successful student should devote the majority of their time and energy to answer choices when approaching Role questions. As you get comfortable with analyzing the structure of the stimulus, it should become second nature to you. I use about 60% of all the time to examine answer choices in Role questions, with the other 40% set aside for reading the question and the stimulus.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The ability to de-abstractify (translating vague and abstract terms that appear in answer choices into their stimulus equivalents) and knowing exactly what they are referring to is one of the most important skills that the advanced student needs to master. If you do not have this ability, often you will end up choosing a wrong answer choice even if you knew what you were looking for. You simply did not understand what the answer choice was trying to say. In other words, advanced students can read through a list of abstract answer choices and know exactly what they are referring to.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Take a look at the following answer choice examples, and see if you can decipher what is it that they are referring to, or even better, try to make up an argument with the components that would function as these answer choices describe:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<section class=\\"exercise\\"><div class=\\"exercise-label\\">Exercise 1</div><div class=\\"question-card\\"><div class=\\"question-label\\">Answer Choice</div><div class=\\"question-text\\">It is used to illustrate the general principle that the argument presupposes</div></div><div class=\\"breakdown-label\\">Answer Choice Breakdown</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Whenever we are faced with an answer choice that looks super abstract, the first thing to do is to always <strong>extract what keywords we can</strong> to try to make sense of the whole thing. Three words stand out in this answer choice: “illustrate”, “general principle”, and “presuppose”.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Now certain words have specific meanings within the LSAT. All three of these words appear repeatedly in LR questions and we need to memorize them:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>Presuppose:</strong> in daily usage, a presupposition can mean either an assumption or something that is necessary. On the LSAT presuppose simply means “require”. (It’s talking about a necessary condition, something that we will soon cover.)</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>Illustrate:</strong> an illustration, as it appears in a LR question, is simply referring to an example/examples</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>General Principle:</strong> we briefly touched upon the nature of principles on the LSAT in the last chapter, and will do so again further down the road. But for now, just know that a principle is a rule on what we should/shouldn’t do; and that it applies to multiple conditions and scenarios.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>For example, “<em>John is always tired in the morning, he should go to bed earlier</em>” is not a principle, we are just talking about one person here. “<em>Those who are tired in the morning should strive to get to bed earlier</em>” is a principle. It’s no longer just about John anymore, it is a recommendation for a certain category of people.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So now there are three requirements that must be met if we were to select this hypothetical answer choice as the correct answer. First, the statement in question must be an example; second, behind this example is a principle on what we should or shouldn’t do; and lastly, this principle is required in order for the author to advance their argument.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Take a look at this example:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><span class=\\"underlined\\">Despite a high paying job, John was extremely stressed out and depressed</span>, so there are intangible things in life that we must pursue to find true happiness.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>John’s situation is the illustration of a general principle; namely that having a lot of money isn’t enough to make you happy. Is this principle required in order for the conclusion to stand? Yes, the conclusion says factors other than money must be considered in order to be happy. The underlined statement satisfies all three requirements and if this was a Role question, this would be the correct answer.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n<section class=\\"exercise\\"><div class=\\"exercise-label\\">Exercise 2</div><div class=\\"question-card\\"><div class=\\"question-label\\">Answer Choice</div><div class=\\"question-text\\">It is an illustration of a premise that is used to support the argument’s conclusion</div></div><div class=\\"breakdown-label\\">Answer Choice Breakdown</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Again, “<strong>Illustration</strong>” means example. So we know this answer choice is referring to an example in the stimulus. Furthermore, it is an illustration of a premise. So when examining this answer choice, we must isolate the premise/premises that appear in the argument and ask ourselves, is the quoted statement an example of what the premise is trying to say?</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>Here is an example to illustrate what this answer choice is trying to say:</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><span class=\\"underlined\\">John has been smoking two packs a day since he was sixteen, last week he was diagnosed with lung cancer.</span> Smoking is a direct cause of lung cancer. We must avoid cigarettes at all costs if we want to stay healthy.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>What is the premise here?</strong> Smoking causes lung cancer. <strong>What is the conclusion?</strong> Don’t smoke. John’s case is an illustration of the causal relationship between smoking and lung cancer. Requirement satisfied.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n<section class=\\"exercise\\"><div class=\\"exercise-label\\">Exercise 3</div><div class=\\"question-card\\"><div class=\\"question-label\\">Answer Choice</div><div class=\\"question-text\\">It is used to counter a consideration that might be taken to undermine the argument’s conclusion</div></div><div class=\\"breakdown-label\\">Answer Choice Breakdown</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So right away, we should realize that “<em>a consideration that might be taken to undermine the argument’s conclusion</em>” is referring to a real or potential counter argument. A counter argument runs contrary to the author’s conclusion. If I say “going to college is a great way to enjoy a higher standard of living down the road,” but you respond with “but many college graduates have to take years to pay back debt”, you are making a counter argument in opposition to me.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Continuing with our example, if I had anticipated your potential objection/counter argument, I can say something along the lines of “college graduates enjoy higher income than their non college counterparts even with the cost of debt factored in.” What I’m doing, essentially, is anticipating your potential objection and shutting you down in advance. That’s what is meant by countering a consideration that might be taken to undermine the argument’s conclusion.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><span class=\\"underlined\\">College graduates make more money than they would if they didn’t go to college, even if they are paying off student loans plus interest.</span> So going to college is a great way to make more money and enjoy a better life.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here, the underlined statement is both a premise as well as a pre-emptive strike against a potential objection, namely that graduates end up with so much debt that going to college doesn’t make sense.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n<section class=\\"exercise\\"><div class=\\"exercise-label\\">Exercise 4</div><div class=\\"question-card\\"><div class=\\"question-label\\">Answer Choice</div><div class=\\"question-text\\">It makes an observation that, according to the argument, is insufficient to justify the claim that the argument concludes is false</div></div><div class=\\"breakdown-label\\">Answer Choice Breakdown</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>A key piece of information we can extract from this answer choice is that there is a <em>claim that the argument concludes is false</em>. In other words, the author of this argument is arguing AGAINST a claim. Someone has made a claim, and the author is saying that claim is false.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So we have two viewpoints in this potential stimulus. The author’s argument is directed against an adversary.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Further, we know that the opponent made an observation in support of their view, but the author is saying that observation is not enough to prove anything, and subsequently goes on to argue that the opponent’s claim is false.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So in order for this answer choice to be correct, the stimulus must satisfy the following requirements: there must be an opposing view, the author must be arguing against this opposing view and declaring it false. The opponent must have provided an observation as a basis of support for their view, and the author must have made the claim that this observation is not enough to demonstrate the truth of the opponent’s view.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s go a step further with a concrete example:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>John:</strong> <span class=\\"underlined\\">Celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera</span>, so they must be the happiest people alive.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>David:</strong> That is not true. Many celebrities suffer from depression and substance abuse. Smiling in front of the camera doesn’t mean a thing, they could simply be putting on a show for the audience.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Does the underlined statement fit the requirement for this answer choice? Yes, David is arguing that John’s claim is false. David also suggests that the observation John makes in support of his claim is insufficient to justify his claim.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n<section class=\\"exercise\\"><div class=\\"exercise-label\\">Exercise 5</div><div class=\\"question-card\\"><div class=\\"question-label\\">Answer Choice</div><div class=\\"question-text\\">It describes a phenomenon for which the argument’s conclusion is offered as an explanation</div></div><div class=\\"breakdown-label\\">Answer Choice Breakdown</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here is another key term that we must familiarize ourselves with: <strong>explanation</strong>.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>Explanation: on the LSAT, the word “explanation” has a very strict and precise meaning, it simply means “cause.”</strong></div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So whenever you see the word “explanation” in an answer choice, check the stimulus for cause - effect reasoning. If it’s not there, then this answer would be wrong.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>What this answer choice is referring to is something we have seen before. We talked about questions where there is a cause and effect relationship in the argument, but instead of the cause being the premise and the effect being the conclusion, the order is reversed. The author describes a phenomenon and concludes what caused it. See PT43-S3-Q23 in the Find the Conclusion chapter if you had forgotten.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>We will reuse our example from the previous chapter:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">Smoking is a leading cause of lung cancer. <span class=\\"underlined\\">John has lung cancer</span>, therefore he must surely have been a heavy smoker.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>John having lung cancer is the <strong>phenomenon</strong>, the author subsequently concludes that it must be smoking which caused his lung cancer. The cause - effect relationship goes like this: smoking → lung cancer, and the cause/explanation is in fact the conclusion in this example.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n<section class=\\"exercise\\"><div class=\\"exercise-label\\">Exercise 6</div><div class=\\"question-card\\"><div class=\\"question-label\\">Answer Choice</div><div class=\\"question-text\\">It is a general principle whose validity the argument questions</div></div><div class=\\"breakdown-label\\">Answer Choice Breakdown</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Again, this answer choice is referring to a general principle, but it’s a general principle that the argument is questioning/attacking. So if we are confronted with this answer choice, we must go back to the stimulus to make sure it satisfies two requirements: one, the quoted statement is indeed a general principle; and two, the author is arguing against the truth of this principle. Here is an example that would match what this answer choice is saying:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">It has been said that <span class=\\"underlined\\">the best way to approach any situation is to be yourself</span>. But this cannot be true. What if you are a selfish, egotistical or horrible person? Surely we must strive to better ourselves and be the best we can be?</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n</section>\\r\\n<section class=\\"exercise\\"><div class=\\"exercise-label\\">Exercise 7</div><div class=\\"question-card\\"><div class=\\"question-label\\">Answer Choice</div><div class=\\"question-text\\">It denies a claim that the argument takes to be assumed in the reasoning it rejects</div></div><div class=\\"breakdown-label\\">Answer Choice Breakdown</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>This one is very similar to example 4. The argument is rejecting another person’s reasoning. So the author is arguing against someone else here. But are they rejecting that view outright? No, the author does something much more nuanced than that. The author thinks the reasoning used by their opponent has made an assumption, and instead of going after the opponent’s argument, our author is going after the opponent’s assumption.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So this answer choice is describing a stimulus in which there’s two arguments, the author’s argument and the opponent’s argument. This particular statement is the author rejecting an assumption made by the opponent’s argument.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>(We will look at the nature of assumptions in a subsequent chapter, as well as in Sufficient and Necessary Assumption question types.)</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>John:</strong> I’ve studied so hard for the finals, I’m definitely going to ace the exam!</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>David:</strong> <span class=\\"underlined\\">Studying hard doesn’t guarantee you will do well.</span> The exams are graded on a curve, and everyone else has studied just as hard.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

  color:var(--muted);\\r\\n    font-size:.84rem;\\r\\n    margin-bottom:2rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .choice-list{\\r\\n    margin:1.25rem 0 2rem;\\r\\n    padding-left:2rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .choice-list li{\\r\\n    margin:1rem 0;\\r\\n    padding-left:.35rem;\\r\\n    font-style:italic;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .choice{\\r\\n    font-style:italic;\\r\\n    font-weight:600;\\r\\n    margin:.9rem 0 1.1rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .definition{\\r\\n    border:1px solid #68645c;\\r\\n    background:var(--panel);\\r\\n    padding:12px 15px;\\r\\n    margin:1rem 0;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .definition strong{\\r\\n    font-family:Arial,Helvetica,sans-serif;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .example{\\r\\n    font-style:italic;\\r\\n    margin:1rem 1.4rem;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .underlined{text-decoration:underline}\\r\\n  .bold{font-weight:700}\\r\\n  .rule{\\r\\n    border:0;\\r\\n    border-top:1px solid var(--line);\\r\\n    margin:2.4rem 0;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  .asterisk{\\r\\n    text-align:center;\\r\\n    font-weight:700;\\r\\n    letter-spacing:.2em;\\r\\n    margin:2rem 0 0;\\r\\n  }\\r\\n  @media(max-width:720px){\\r\\n    main{margin:0;padding:30px 20px 50px}\\r\\n    h1{font-size:1.7rem}\\r\\n  }\\r\\n</style>\\r\\n</head>\\r\\n<body>\\r\\n<main>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h1>Role Question Answer Choices (The Hardest Thing about Role Questions)</h1>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>While the way in which we read the stimulus of any Role question is similar to how we approach Find the Conclusion questions, the answer choices we are faced with in Role questions are much more difficult. Life would be so simple if all the answer choices were just asking us if the statement in question is a premise or conclusion, but the test makers love to describe a relatively straightforward concept in abstract and vague language in Role question answer choices.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>As a result, the successful student should devote the majority of their time and energy to answer choices when approaching Role questions. As you get comfortable with analyzing the structure of the stimulus, it should become second nature to you. I use about 60% of all the time to examine answer choices in Role questions, with the other 40% set aside for reading the question and the stimulus.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The ability to de-abstractify (translating vague and abstract terms that appear in answer choices into their stimulus equivalents) and knowing exactly what they are referring to is one of the most important skills that the advanced student needs to master. If you do not have this ability, often you will end up choosing a wrong answer choice even if you knew what you were looking for. You simply did not understand what the answer choice was trying to say. In other words, advanced students can read through a list of abstract answer choices and know exactly what they are referring to.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Take a look at the following answer choice examples, and see if you can decipher what is it that they are referring to, or even better, try to make up an argument with the components that would function as these answer choices describe:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<ol class=\\"choice-list\\">\\r\\n<li>It is used to illustrate the general principle that the argument presupposes</li>\\r\\n<li>It is an illustration of a premise that is used to support the argument’s conclusion</li>\\r\\n<li>It is used to counter a consideration that might be taken to undermine the argument’s conclusion</li>\\r\\n<li>It makes an observation that, according to the argument, is insufficient to justify the claim that the argument concludes is false</li>\\r\\n<li>It describes a phenomenon for which the argument’s conclusion is offered as an explanation</li>\\r\\n<li>It is a general principle whose validity the argument questions</li>\\r\\n<li>It denies a claim that the argument takes to be assumed in the reasoning it rejects</li>\\r\\n<li>It is a claim for which no justification is provided but that is required to establish the argument’s main conclusion</li>\\r\\n<li>It is a claim for which justification is provided and that, if true, establishes the truth of the argument’s main conclusion</li>\\r\\n<li>It is what the author’s argument purports to explain</li>\\r\\n<li>It is a hypothesis that the argument attempts to undermine by calling into question the sufficiency of the evidence</li>\\r\\n<li>It is the conclusion of the argument as a whole but is not the only explicitly stated conclusion in the argument</li>\\r\\n<li>It is a statement that the argument is intended to support but is not the conclusion of the argument as a whole</li>\\r\\n<li>It is a statement for which some evidence is provided and that itself is offered as support for the conclusion of the argument as a whole</li>\\r\\n<li>It is the conclusion of the argument as a whole and is supported by another statement for which support is offered</li>\\r\\n</ol>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Yep, that was brutal. You probably had no idea what many of these even meant. Not to worry, let’s break them down one by one:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>1. It is used to illustrate the general principle that the argument presupposes</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Whenever we are faced with an answer choice that looks super abstract, the first thing to do is to always <strong>extract what keywords we can</strong> to try to make sense of the whole thing. Three words stand out in this answer choice: “illustrate”, “general principle”, and “presuppose”.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Now certain words have specific meanings within the LSAT. All three of these words appear repeatedly in LR questions and we need to memorize them:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>Presuppose:</strong> in daily usage, a presupposition can mean either an assumption or something that is necessary. On the LSAT presuppose simply means “require”. (It’s talking about a necessary condition, something that we will soon cover.)</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>Illustrate:</strong> an illustration, as it appears in a LR question, is simply referring to an example/examples</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>General Principle:</strong> we briefly touched upon the nature of principles on the LSAT in the last chapter, and will do so again further down the road. But for now, just know that a principle is a rule on what we should/shouldn’t do; and that it applies to multiple conditions and scenarios.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>For example, “<em>John is always tired in the morning, he should go to bed earlier</em>” is not a principle, we are just talking about one person here. “<em>Those who are tired in the morning should strive to get to bed earlier</em>” is a principle. It’s no longer just about John anymore, it is a recommendation for a certain category of people.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So now there are three requirements that must be met if we were to select this hypothetical answer choice as the correct answer. First, the statement in question must be an example; second, behind this example is a principle on what we should or shouldn’t do; and lastly, this principle is required in order for the author to advance their argument.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Take a look at this example:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><span class=\\"underlined\\">Despite a high paying job, John was extremely stressed out and depressed</span>, so there are intangible things in life that we must pursue to find true happiness.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>John’s situation is the illustration of a general principle; namely that having a lot of money isn’t enough to make you happy. Is this principle required in order for the conclusion to stand? Yes, the conclusion says factors other than money must be considered in order to be happy. The underlined statement satisfies all three requirements and if this was a Role question, this would be the correct answer.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>2. It is an illustration of a premise that is used to support the argument’s conclusion</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Again, “<strong>Illustration</strong>” means example. So we know this answer choice is referring to an example in the stimulus. Furthermore, it is an illustration of a premise. So when examining this answer choice, we must isolate the premise/premises that appear in the argument and ask ourselves, is the quoted statement an example of what the premise is trying to say?</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>Here is an example to illustrate what this answer choice is trying to say:</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><span class=\\"underlined\\">John has been smoking two packs a day since he was sixteen, last week he was diagnosed with lung cancer.</span> Smoking is a direct cause of lung cancer. We must avoid cigarettes at all costs if we want to stay healthy.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>What is the premise here?</strong> Smoking causes lung cancer. <strong>What is the conclusion?</strong> Don’t smoke. John’s case is an illustration of the causal relationship between smoking and lung cancer. Requirement satisfied.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>3. It is used to counter a consideration that might be taken to undermine the argument’s conclusion</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So right away, we should realize that “<em>a consideration that might be taken to undermine the argument’s conclusion</em>” is referring to a real or potential counter argument. A counter argument runs contrary to the author’s conclusion. If I say “going to college is a great way to enjoy a higher standard of living down the road,” but you respond with “but many college graduates have to take years to pay back debt”, you are making a counter argument in opposition to me.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Continuing with our example, if I had anticipated your potential objection/counter argument, I can say something along the lines of “college graduates enjoy higher income than their non college counterparts even with the cost of debt factored in.” What I’m doing, essentially, is anticipating your potential objection and shutting you down in advance. That’s what is meant by countering a consideration that might be taken to undermine the argument’s conclusion.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><span class=\\"underlined\\">College graduates make more money than they would if they didn’t go to college, even if they are paying off student loans plus interest.</span> So going to college is a great way to make more money and enjoy a better life.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here, the underlined statement is both a premise as well as a pre-emptive strike against a potential objection, namely that graduates end up with so much debt that going to college doesn’t make sense.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>4. It makes an observation that, according to the argument, is insufficient to justify the claim that the argument concludes is false</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>A key piece of information we can extract from this answer choice is that there is a <em>claim that the argument concludes is false</em>. In other words, the author of this argument is arguing AGAINST a claim. Someone has made a claim, and the author is saying that claim is false.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So we have two viewpoints in this potential stimulus. The author’s argument is directed against an adversary.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Further, we know that the opponent made an observation in support of their view, but the author is saying that observation is not enough to prove anything, and subsequently goes on to argue that the opponent’s claim is false.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So in order for this answer choice to be correct, the stimulus must satisfy the following requirements: there must be an opposing view, the author must be arguing against this opposing view and declaring it false. The opponent must have provided an observation as a basis of support for their view, and the author must have made the claim that this observation is not enough to demonstrate the truth of the opponent’s view.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s go a step further with a concrete example:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>John:</strong> <span class=\\"underlined\\">Celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera</span>, so they must be the happiest people alive.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>David:</strong> That is not true. Many celebrities suffer from depression and substance abuse. Smiling in front of the camera doesn’t mean a thing, they could simply be putting on a show for the audience.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Does the underlined statement fit the requirement for this answer choice? Yes, David is arguing that John’s claim is false. David also suggests that the observation John makes in support of his claim is insufficient to justify his claim.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>5. It describes a phenomenon for which the argument’s conclusion is offered as an explanation</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here is another key term that we must familiarize ourselves with: <strong>explanation</strong>.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>Explanation: on the LSAT, the word “explanation” has a very strict and precise meaning, it simply means “cause.”</strong></div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So whenever you see the word “explanation” in an answer choice, check the stimulus for cause - effect reasoning. If it’s not there, then this answer would be wrong.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>What this answer choice is referring to is something we have seen before. We talked about questions where there is a cause and effect relationship in the argument, but instead of the cause being the premise and the effect being the conclusion, the order is reversed. The author describes a phenomenon and concludes what caused it. See PT43-S3-Q23 in the Find the Conclusion chapter if you had forgotten.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>We will reuse our example from the previous chapter:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">Smoking is a leading cause of lung cancer. <span class=\\"underlined\\">John has lung cancer</span>, therefore he must surely have been a heavy smoker.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>John having lung cancer is the <strong>phenomenon</strong>, the author subsequently concludes that it must be smoking which caused his lung cancer. The cause - effect relationship goes like this: smoking → lung cancer, and the cause/explanation is in fact the conclusion in this example.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>6. It is a general principle whose validity the argument questions</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Again, this answer choice is referring to a general principle, but it’s a general principle that the argument is questioning/attacking. So if we are confronted with this answer choice, we must go back to the stimulus to make sure it satisfies two requirements: one, the quoted statement is indeed a general principle; and two, the author is arguing against the truth of this principle. Here is an example that would match what this answer choice is saying:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">It has been said that <span class=\\"underlined\\">the best way to approach any situation is to be yourself</span>. But this cannot be true. What if you are a selfish, egotistical or horrible person? Surely we must strive to better ourselves and be the best we can be?</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>7. It denies a claim that the argument takes to be assumed in the reasoning it rejects</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>This one is very similar to example 4. The argument is rejecting another person’s reasoning. So the author is arguing against someone else here. But are they rejecting that view outright? No, the author does something much more nuanced than that. The author thinks the reasoning used by their opponent has made an assumption, and instead of going after the opponent’s argument, our author is going after the opponent’s assumption.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So this answer choice is describing a stimulus in which there’s two arguments, the author’s argument and the opponent’s argument. This particular statement is the author rejecting an assumption made by the opponent’s argument.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>(We will look at the nature of assumptions in a subsequent chapter, as well as in Sufficient and Necessary Assumption question types.)</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>John:</strong> I’ve studied so hard for the finals, I’m definitely going to ace the exam!</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\"><strong>David:</strong> <span class=\\"underlined\\">Studying hard doesn’t guarantee you will do well.</span> The exams are graded on a curve, and everyone else has studied just as hard.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>8. It is a claim for which no justification is provided but that is required to establish the argument’s main conclusion</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>There are two important pieces of information in this answer choice that can help us determine whether it’s the correct answer. First, it is “required” to establish the argument’s main conclusion. That means it’s necessary in order for the main conclusion to be valid. So there’s no way this answer choice is talking about the conclusion itself, it’s therefore either a premise or an intermediate conclusion.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Second, the answer choice says it’s a claim for which no justification is provided. Remember what we said about how to differentiate between premises and intermediate conclusions in the previous chapter? Premises support the conclusion but do not have any other statements supporting them, whereas intermediate conclusions both support the conclusion and are supported by premises. So when we have a statement which supports the conclusion but is not supported by others, it can only mean one thing: we are talking about a premise here.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So we are essentially talking about a premise that is required to establish the truth of the conclusion. In other words, if the premise was untrue, the conclusion would not stand. Take a look at the following example for more clarity:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">I found an Olympic gold medal in the swim team locker room. It must have originally belonged to John. John is always losing things and <span class=\\"underlined\\">he participated in the Olympics.</span></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>John’s participation in the Olympics is a requirement for the example’s conclusion to stand. In order for John to be the original owner of the medal, he MUST have been a participant at least.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>John’s participation in the Olympics is also a premise that supports the example argument’s conclusion.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>9. It is a claim for which justification is provided and that, if true, establishes the truth of the argument’s main conclusion</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Very similar to the example listed above, but with two important caveats. This answer choice HAS justification provided, while supporting the main conclusion, so it’s an intermediate conclusion. It also <strong>guarantees/establishes</strong> the truth of the conclusion.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">All practicing lawyers must have passed the bar. John is a partner at a prestigious law firm and therefore <span class=\\"underlined\\">he is a practicing lawyer</span>, so he must have passed the bar.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>John being a practicing lawyer is supported by the fact that he is a partner at a law firm. This satisfies the intermediate conclusion requirement. Does John being a practicing lawyer establish the fact that he has passed the bar? Yes, it is mentioned in the first statement/premise that all practicing lawyers must have passed the bar.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>10. It is what the author’s argument purports to explain</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>This is identical to example 5: the author describes an effect/result/phenomenon, and argues for a specific cause.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>11. It is a hypothesis that the argument attempts to undermine by calling into question the sufficiency of the evidence</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>What is a hypothesis?</strong> In science, a hypothesis is offered by scientists to try to explain a phenomenon in the physical world or universe. Why did different types of birds have physical features that are adapted to their physical environments? Darwin hypothesized that it is due to the fact that species can change over time, adapting to their environments via physical modifications generation after generation. Darwin came up with a hypothesis, which today we know as The Theory of Evolution, that has been supported by evidence gathered both by him and subsequent scientists. In other words, a hypothesis is simply a posited causal explanation of a phenomenon, explaining WHY something is the way it is.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"definition\\"><strong>On the LSAT, the word “hypothesis” can be understood as a “potential explanation/potential causation”; it usually appears in science/medicine themed LR stimuli.</strong></div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So what is this answer choice saying? The argument is attempting to undermine this hypothesis. It does so by saying that the evidence out there is not enough to support such a hypothesis. So when we are going through answer choices and come to this, we must make sure that there is a hypothesis present in the stimulus (the quoted statement being that hypothesis), the author is challenging this hypothesis, and the author does so by suggesting that the available evidence is not enough to support this hypothesis.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>12. It is the conclusion of the argument as a whole but is not the only explicitly stated conclusion in the argument</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Two pieces of key information here. The first is straightforward: we are talking about the main conclusion here. But note that it “<em>is not the only explicitly stated conclusion in the argument</em>”. That can only mean one thing: we have an intermediate conclusion in the argument as well. So if confronted with this answer choice, make sure that one, the statement we are talking about is in fact the main conclusion of the argument; and two, the argument contains a separate intermediate conclusion.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>13. It is a statement that the argument is intended to support but is not the conclusion of the argument as a whole</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Something that is supported by the argument but is not the main conclusion, that’s an intermediate conclusion we are talking about here.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>14. It is a statement for which some evidence is provided and that itself is offered as support for the conclusion of the argument as a whole</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Same as above. The statement in question is supported by support, and supports the main conclusion. This is also the intermediate conclusion.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>15. It is the conclusion of the argument as a whole and is supported by another statement for which support is offered</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Same as example 12: the statement referred to by the answer choice is the main conclusion, and is supported by another statement for which support is offered. So the main conclusion is supported by an intermediate conclusion. When faced with an answer choice like this, make sure it is indeed the main conclusion that the question is asking us to analyze, and make sure the argument contains an intermediate conclusion.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"asterisk\\">***</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n</main>\\r\\n</body>\\r\\n</html>\\r\\n"}'}]

Put It Together\n    - button "Final Set":\n      - generic: Final Set\n    - button "Reference":\n      - text: Reference\n- main:\n  - button "Module 3":\n    - text: Module 3\n  - paragraph: Answer choice 1 of 15\n  - progressbar "Answer choices completed"\n  - heading "What would this answer choice describe?" [level=1]\n  - paragraph: It is used to illustrate the general principle that the argument presupposes\n  - generic: What must be present for this answer choice to be correct?\n  - textbox "What must be present for this answer choice to be correct?" [disabled]: The statement must be an example of a general principle, and the argument must require that principle.\n  - generic: Write your own example argument. Identify the statement this answer choice describes.\n  - textbox "Write your own example argument. Identify the statement this answer choice describes." [disabled]: Maya earned a promotion but remained unhappy. So we should pursue things other than career success to be happy. The statement about Maya illustrates that career success alone is not enough for happiness, which this argument requires.\n  - heading "The breakdown" [level=2]\n  - paragraph:\n    - text: Whenever we are faced with an answer choice that looks super abstract, the first thing to do is to always\n    - strong: extract what keywords we can\n    - text: "to try to make sense of the whole thing. Three words stand out in this answer choice: “illustrate”, “general principle”, and “presuppose”."\n  - paragraph: "Now certain words have specific meanings within the LSAT. All three of these words appear repeatedly in LR questions and we need to memorize them:"\n  - strong: "Presuppose:"\n  - text: in daily usage, a presupposition can mean either an assumption or something that is necessary. On the LSAT presuppose simply means “require”. (It’s talking about a necessary condition, something that we will soon cover.)\n  - strong: "Illustrate:"\n  - text: an illustration, as it appears in a LR question, is simply referring to an example/examples\n  - strong: "General Principle:"\n  - text: we briefly touched upon the nature of principles on the LSAT in the last chapter, and will do so again further down the road. But for now, just know that a principle is a rule on what we should/shouldn’t do; and that it applies to multiple conditions and scenarios.\n  - paragraph:\n    - text: For example, “\n    - emphasis: John is always tired in the morning, he should go to bed earlier\n    - text: ” is not a principle, we are just talking about one person here. “\n    - emphasis: Those who are tired in the morning should strive to get to bed earlier\n    - text: ” is a principle. It’s no longer just about John anymore, it is a recommendation for a certain category of people.\n  - paragraph: So now there are three requirements that must be met if we were to select this hypothetical answer choice as the correct answer. First, the statement in question must be an example; second, behind this example is a principle on what we should or shouldn’t do; and lastly, this principle is required in order for the author to advance their argument.\n  - paragraph: "Take a look at this example:"\n  - paragraph: Despite a high paying job, John was extremely stressed out and depressed, so there are intangible things in life that we must pursue to find true happiness.\n  - paragraph: John’s situation is the illustration of a general principle; namely that having a lot of money isn’t enough to make you happy. Is this principle required in order for the conclusion to stand? Yes, the conclusion says factors other than money must be considered in order to be happy. The underlined statement satisfies all three requirements and if this was a Role question, this would be the correct answer.\n  - paragraph: Compare your work with the breakdown. Your responses are saved as practice, without a correctness score.\n  - button "Next answer choice"'}]

This skill drives the 21st MCP server.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When to reach for it\\r\\n\\r\\n- The user asks to add/build a UI element: \\"add a pricing section\\", \\"I need a nice navbar\\", \\"make a testimonials block\\".\\r\\n- The user wants options or inspiration before committing to a design.\\r\\n- The user needs a company logo in JSX/TSX (`search_logo`).\\r\\n- The user wants brand-new UI generated from a description (`generate`).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Workflow: install an existing component (default path)\\r\\n\\r\\nPrefer real catalog components over writing UI from scratch — they ship with dependencies, demos, and responsive/dark-mode support.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. `search` with a short natural query (e.g. \\"pricing table\\", \\"animated hero\\"). Results include names, descriptions, previews, and install ids.\\r\\n2. Pick the best match for the user\'s stack and style; show the user 1–3 top candidates if the choice isn\'t obvious.\\r\\n3. `get_component` to fetch the full code and metadata for the chosen item.\\r\\n4. Install into the project the way the result instructs (shadcn-style registry add or by writing the returned files), then wire it into the page and adapt tokens/props to the project\'s design system.\\r\\n\\r\\nNotes:\\r\\n- Free tier allows catalog search and 2 component installs per day; paid components return code only after unlock.\\r\\n- Match the project\'s existing conventions (Tailwind config, cn helper, component folder layout) when integrating.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Workflow: generate new UI\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen nothing in the catalog fits or the user explicitly wants custom UI:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Check `get_usage.aiGenerationEnabled` and the current `tools/list`. Paid component access alone does not enable hosted AI. Missing or unknown status is not permission to generate.\\r\\n2. With AI off, use `search` and `get_component`, then implement or adapt the UI with your own coding agent. Do not call generation tools, legacy builder/refiner aliases, or the CLI as a workaround.\\r\\n3. When AI is enabled and `generate` is listed, call it with the purpose, layout, content, style, and stack constraints. It consumes AI credits and returns a preview URL; open that URL to view the generation.\\r\\n4. For existing sketch drafts, use `get_generation` and `get_take` to read their code. These reads remain available with AI off.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf a cached call returns `ai_subscription_required`, stop generation attempts. After enabling AI, refresh the tool list or reconnect before trying again. The entitlement flag does not report the remaining credit balance.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Logos\\r\\n\\r\\n`search_logo` returns brand logos as ready-to-paste JSX/TSX — one brand per call.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Setup and auth\\r\\n\\r\\nThe server is HTTP MCP at `https://21st.dev/api/mcp`, authenticated with the `x-api-key` header. Keys are free and instant at https://21st.dev/mcp. If a call fails with an auth error, tell the user to grab a key there and set it in the plugin configuration (variable `API_KEY_21ST`).\\r\\n\\r\\nLegacy Magic tool names (`21st_magic_component_builder`, `logo_search`, ...) are still accepted server-side, but prefer the current names: `search`, `get_component`, `generate`, `get_inspiration`, `search_logo`.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"tools":[{"name":"mcp__21st__search","description":"Search the 21st.dev catalog across ALL entity types - React/shadcn components, themes, and templates - returning lightweight metadata ONLY (name, description, preview image, author, url/install, id, and price for templates). Use `type` to scope to one kind, or \'all\' (default) to search everything. FREE. Retrieval differs by kind: for a component result call get_component with its `id` (that id is a demo id) for the PAID code; for a theme call get_theme with its `id` (a uuid) for the free CSS; templates have no code to fetch - open their `url`. NOTE: `author`/`mine`/`liked` bypass ranking and return a plain recency-ordered list (query/sort/tag/color are ignored when any of them is set); component listings via `mine`/`author` only ever show PUBLIC components - your own private/team components are discoverable via list_team_components, not search. Pass `runtime: \'remotion\'` (optionally `kind`, `aspect`) to search 21st Video blocks, Remotion components for programmatic video; without it results are UI only. Returns plain data, no picker UI - use this for your own lookups. If the USER should see a visual list of options and pick one, use `search_picker` instead (identical params, same results, but renders an inline picker on hosts that support it). This tool is part of plugin `21st.dev`.\\n\\nexec tool declaration:\\n```ts\\ndeclare const tools: { mcp__21st__search(args: {\\n  // runtime \'remotion\' only: blocks that support this aspect ratio.\\n  aspect?: \\"16:9\\" | \\"9:16\\" | \\"1:1\\";\\n  // Filter to items by this username.\\n  author?: string;\\n  // Optional primary-color bucket filter (themes), e.g. \'blue\', \'violet\'.\\n  color?: string;\\n  // Templates only: true = free (price 0), false = paid. No effect elsewhere.\\n  free?: boolean;\\n  // runtime \'remotion\' only: the video block kind.\\n  kind?: \\"element\\" | \\"scene\\" | \\"background\\" | \\"transition\\" | \\"captions\\" | \\"overlay-fx\\" | \\"template\\";\\n  // Filter to items the caller has bookmarked.\\n  liked?: boolean;\\n  // Max results (default 8).\\n  limit?: number;\\n  // Filter to the signed-in caller\'s own items.\\n  mine?: boolean;\\n  // What you need, e.g. \'pricing table\', \'dark violet theme\', \'saas template\'.\\n  query: string;\\n  // \'ui\' (default): React/shadcn components, themes, templates. \'remotion\': 21st Video blocks (Remotion components) only. A bare \'video\' query stays UI unless you pass \'remotion\'.\\n  runtime?: \\"ui\\" | \\"remotion\\";\\n  // Themes: recommended|popular|newest. Templates: recommended|date|price. No effect on components (always relevance-ranked).\\n  sort?: \\"recommended\\" | \\"popular\\" | \\"newest\\" | \\"date\\" | \\"price\\";\\n  // Optional tag slug filter (templates).\\n  tag?: string;\\n  // Which catalog to search. Default \'all\'.\\n  type?: \\"component\\" | \\"theme\\" | \\"template\\" | \\"all\\";\\n}): Promise<CallToolResult<{\\n  // Matching catalog entities (empty array when nothing matched).\\n  results?: Array<{\\n  // 21st Video blocks only: the first declared aspect (16:9 | 9:16 | 1:1), the card\'s native shape; absent on UI hits.\\n  aspect?: string;\\n  // 21st Video blocks only: every aspect the block supports; absent on UI hits.\\n  aspects?: Array<string>;\\n  // Author username, null when unknown.\\n  author?: string | null;\\n  // Author avatar URL; may be absent or null.\\n  authorImage?: string | null;\\n  // Description, null when absent.\\n  description?: string | null;\\n  // Component demo id (number), theme uuid (string), or template id (number).\\n  id?: number | string;\\n  // shadcn install command (components only; may be absent).\\n  installCommand?: string;\\n  // 21st Video blocks only: element | scene | background | transition | captions | overlay-fx | template; null when unknown; absent on UI hits.\\n  kind?: string | null;\\n  // Library name when source is team (may be absent).\\n  libraryName?: string;\\n  // Display name.\\n  name?: string;\\n  // Preview image URL, null when absent.\\n  previewUrl?: string | null;\\n  // Template price in USD, 0 = free (templates only; may be absent).\\n  price?: number;\\n  // \\"remotion\\" on 21st Video blocks (install with the /r/video/ command into a Remotion project); absent on UI hits.\\n  runtime?: string;\\n  // Provenance: catalog | team | bookmark (may be absent).\\n  source?: string;\\n  // Team name when source is team (may be absent).\\n  teamName?: string;\\n  // Entity kind: component | theme | template.\\n  type?: string;\\n  // Public 21st.dev page URL; may be absent.\\n  url?: string;\\n  // Optional hover-video URL (components); may be absent or null.\\n  videoUrl?: string | null;\\n  [key: string]: unknown;\\n}>;\\n  [key: string]: unknown;\\n}>>; };\\n```"},{"name":"mcp__21st__list_team_components","description":"List the components in a team\'s library, as the uniform search metadata shape. Requires membership. Pass teamId (from list_teams); optionally libraryId (from list_team_libraries) to scope to one library. Free metadata; use get_component for code. This tool is part of plugin `21st.dev`.\\n\\nexec tool declaration:\\n```ts\\ndeclare const tools: { mcp__21st__list_team_components(args: {\\n  // Optional: scope to one library (registry) id.\\n  libraryId?: string;\\n  // Max results (default 50).\\n  limit?: number;\\n  // The team id (uuid) from list_teams.\\n  teamId: string;\\n}): Promise<CallToolResult<{\\n  // Components in the team library, uniform entity shape (empty array when none). Note: this path omits videoUrl/authorImage.\\n  results?: Array<{\\n  // Author username, null when unknown.\\n  author?: string | null;\\n  // Description, null when absent.\\n  description?: string | null;\\n  // The demo id (number) for get_component.\\n  id?: number | string;\\n  // shadcn install command (empty string when it cannot be built).\\n  installCommand?: string;\\n  // Component name (\'Untitled component\' fallback).\\n  name?: string;\\n  // Preview image URL, null when absent.\\n  previewUrl?: string | null;\\n  // Always \'component\'.\\n  type?: string;\\n  // Public 21st.dev component page URL; may be absent.\\n  url?: string;\\n  [key: string]: unknown;\\n}>;\\n  [key: string]: unknown;\\n}>>; };\\n```"}]}'}]
